Furthermore, we introduce "Detailed Visual Conditioning", which uses visual conditions encoded by a VAE to guide the generation process, thereby supporting additional generation tasks that require reference images. Extensive experiments demonstrate that Omni-RS performs exceptionally well in understanding tasks and has achieved outstanding results in a variety of generative tasks.</p></details> |  |
| **[Benchmarking Multimodal Large Language Models for Zero-Shot Human-Object Interaction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18161v1)** | 2026-10-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) have demonstrated strong zero-shot reasoning across diverse domains, but their potential for Human-Object Interaction (HOI) remains unexplored. Unlike traditional HOI approaches that rely on extensive labeled datasets and fixed interaction taxonomies, MLLMs offer flexible reasoning, making them well-suited for dynamic, real-world scenarios. In this work, we present the first systematic study of MLLMs for HOI recognition and detection within a pipeline that combines an external object detector for entity localization with zero-shot multimodal reasoning for interaction understanding. We introduce a benchmark framework for principled zero-shot evaluation, consisting of (i) a unified taxonomy of zero-shot prompting strategies, and (ii) a methodology that transforms token-level logits into interaction scores, enabling fair comparison with specialized HOI models under standard metrics. To support prompt-based evaluation, we additionally construct new question-answer annotations for standard HOI datasets, transforming them into a format suitable for MLLMs. Building on this, we propose two visual prompting strategies, namely Objects as Context and Black Other Objects, that guide MLLMs to target objects while preserving scene context. Experiments on HICO, HICO-DET, and V-COCO demonstrate state-of-the-art zero-shot performance, achieving 53.50 mAP on HICO and 23.69 mAP on HICO-DET. Overall, our work provides a principled benchmarking framework for MLLMs in HOI, opening the door to new models and datasets and enabling flexible open-world interaction understanding.</p></details> |  |
| **[Open-MMUnlearning: Unifying Methods and Evaluation for MLLM Unlearning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.10358v2)** | 2026-10-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>As multimodal large language models (MLLMs) become more capable and widely deployed, concerns about privacy and safety have become increasingly pressing. Machine unlearning offers one approach to addressing these concerns by removing designated information from trained models while preserving unrelated capabilities. However, fragmented implementations and evaluation protocols, incomplete robustness testing, and limited understanding of metric reliability make progress in MLLM unlearning difficult to assess systematically. We introduce Open-MMUnlearning, an open-source, extensible framework that integrates target-model preparation, multimodal data processing, unlearning, and evaluation through shared interfaces and structured configurations. The framework supports five benchmarks spanning privacy, safety, and copyright, eight MLLMs from four model families, and twelve unlearning methods. Its evaluation suite jointly assesses forgetting effectiveness, retained utility, and robustness to model interventions, adversarial inputs, and membership inference attacks. Using a common evaluation protocol, we compare ten representative unlearning methods. In this comparison, GD and MIP-Editor tie for the highest overall score: GD achieves the highest Forget Quality, while MIP-Editor preserves more Model Utility. We further introduce a metric meta-evaluation protocol that tests faithfulness using models with controlled exposure to target knowledge and robustness under quantization and relearning. Among the thirteen evaluated metrics, BLEU achieves the highest aggregate reliability score. KS-Test attains the highest faithfulness AUC but performs less well on robustness. Together, the framework and these findings support reproducible comparison of MLLM unlearning methods and systematic assessment of evaluation reliability.</p></details> |  |
| **[ManipShield: A Unified Framework for Image Manipulation Detection, Localization and Explanation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.14259v4)** | 2026-10-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>With the rapid advancement of generative models, powerful image editing methods now enable diverse and highly realistic image manipulations that far surpass traditional deepfake techniques, posing new challenges for manipulation detection. Existing image manipulation detection and localization (IMDL) benchmarks suffer from limited content diversity, narrow generative-model coverage, and insufficient interpretability, which hinders the generalization and explanation capabilities of current manipulation detection methods. To address these limitations, we introduce \textbf{ManipBench}, a large-scale benchmark for image manipulation detection and localization focusing on AI-edited images. Extensive experiments on ManipBench and several public datasets demonstrate that ManipShield achieves state-of-the-art performance and exhibits strong generality to unseen manipulation models. | **[MicroCLIP: Connecting Text and Microscopy Images via Contrastive Language--Image Pretraining](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17866v1)** | 2026-10-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Understanding fluorescence microscopy images is fundamental to automating biological research. However, existing methods typically learn predefined concepts from closed-set data, limiting transfer across tasks and biological contexts. In this work, we introduce MicroCLIP, a vision-language model that learns generalizable cellular and subcellular representations from natural language supervision for open-set image classification and retrieval. To pretrain MicroCLIP, we construct MicroPool-2M, a dataset of approximately 2.5 million fluorescence microscopy image-text pairs spanning diverse cellular structures, cell lines, and imaging conditions. Aligning microscopy images with biological concepts in natural language is challenging because most images lack descriptive captions and biological knowledge annotations. We therefore progressively establish image-text associations at three levels to provide MicroCLIP with richer semantic supervision: (1) category-level alignment links images to standardized labels defined by subcellular structure-cell-line combinations; (2) knowledge-level alignment incorporates biological priors derived from these labels to capture relationships across species and cell types; and (3) detail-level alignment uses a multimodal large language model to recaption and verify image-text pairs at scale, emphasizing fine-grained visual characteristics. Extensive experiments demonstrate that MicroCLIP achieves leading performance in zero-shot classification and text-image retrieval on microscopy images. It retrieves images with specific cellular characteristics from thousands of candidates and generalizes to unseen biological concepts without additional supervision. These results highlight MicroCLIP's potential as a foundation model for fluorescence microscopy.</p></details> |  |
| **[DVTR-KD: Distilling Vision-Language Models via Intermediate Visual Token Reconstruction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17853v1)** | 2026-10-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) have achieved strong performance on diverse vision-language tasks, but their computational demands limit deployment under constrained budgets, particularly on edge devices. Many works focus on Knowledge Distillation (KD) and try to transfer knowledge from powerful teacher models to students. In addition to reducing model size, visual token compression provides a complementary route to efficiency, but introduces mismatched visual token counts that complicate direct token-wise knowledge transfer. To mitigate this, we propose DVTR-KD, a knowledge distillation framework based on \textbf{D}ense \textbf{V}isual \textbf{T}oken \textbf{R}econstruction, which reconstructs the teacher's dense visual tokens from the student's compressed tokens. We reconstruct visual states after the final LLM layer and at the input to an intermediate layer identified as important for visual-information readout. At the intermediate layer, reconstruction is supervised through the teacher's response predictions rather than by direct token-wise KL or MSE matching. An auxiliary attention-readout loss complements this response-level supervision by aligning how the student and the original teacher integrate contextual information when predicting responses. Evaluations on diverse benchmarks demonstrate that DVTR-KD improves the performance of token-compressed students while retaining the inference efficiency afforded by visual token compression.</p></details> |  |
| **[What Makes a Good Visual Representation for Spatial Understanding in MLLMs?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17844v1)** | 2026-10-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Spatial understanding is a necessary step in moving multimodal large language models (MLLMs) toward reasoning about the 3D physical world. Their visual pretraining data, however, is almost entirely 2D, so the visual tokens processed by MLLMs often lack inherent 3D structural information. A growing number of methods inject such structure by transferring representations from external vision or geometry models, and collectively establish that such transfer helps. Behind these methods lies a largely underexplored question: what makes a visual representation good for spatial understanding? To answer it, we first separate what is transferred from how it is transferred, and then trace the resulting differences back to properties of the representations themselves. What we transfer comes from 3D geometry models, self-supervised image and video models, vision-language models, and generative models, in the form of latent features or explicit depth and point maps. We transfer it either by distillation, using it as a supervision target, or by fusion, feeding it as an additional input. We find that a representation's 3D structure correlates strongly with how much it helps, measured by cross-view consistency for latent features and by depth and reconstruction accuracy for explicit geometry. We further show that the form of a representation dictates the mechanism it needs. Latent features gain as much from fusion as from distillation; explicit geometry gains little from fusion, and most of its distillation gain depends on a geometry decoding head. Guided by these findings, we distill both latent features and explicit geometry into a single MLLM. Taking only RGB video as input, it consistently improves over its base model on both static 3D and dynamic 4D spatial reasoning benchmarks.</p></details> | <details><summary>Exten...</summary><p>Extended version of Neurips 2025 paper: https://arxiv.org/abs/2506.01946</p></details> |
| **[When, Where, and Why: Hierarchical Causal Explainability for Video Face Understanding with MLLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17791v1)** | 2026-10-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Understanding facial behaviour in video requires answering three fundamental questions: when a key facial expression occurs, where the model attends on the face at that moment, and why that evidence is necessary rather than merely correlated. First, we introduce ExplainFace, a weakly supervised video-text corpus for facial behaviour understanding, automatically constructed from CelebV-HQ videos and validated through human evaluation and a two-model audit (at most 2.3% contradictions), demonstrating consistent gains across two backbone architectures. Second, H-TCC (Hierarchical Temporal Spatial Causal Concepts), which finds the key temporal moments (When), the facial regions (Where), and verifies its causal necessity (Why) through targeted occlusion. | **[IoU-PD: IoU-Aware Privileged Distillation for Visual Grounding with Multimodal Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15732v4)** | 2026-10-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Visual grounding with multimodal large language models is commonly formulated as autoregressive coordinate generation, where a model outputs bounding-box coordinates as text given an image and a referring-expression prompt. We propose IoU-PD, an IoU-aware privileged distillation method for coordinate-generating multimodal large language models. IoU-PD uses ground-truth boxes not only as coordinate targets, but also as privileged training-time guidance. During training, the student receives the original image and prompt, while a frozen teacher receives a box-marked image and an augmented prompt that indicates the marked region. The student is trained with a supervised fine-tuning anchor and a privileged distillation loss whose token weights reflect both geometric importance and teacher reliability. At inference time, IoU-PD requires no box overlay, privileged hint, teacher branch, or additional prediction module. Experiments on standard referring-expression grounding benchmarks show consistent region-level improvements over strong coordinate-generating baselines, demonstrating that ground-truth boxes can provide useful privileged guidance beyond serving as coordinate labels. Project page: https://xyzzzh.github.io/IoU-PD/</p></details> | <details><summary>16 pa...</summary><p>16 pages, 7 figures, 11 tables</p></details> |
| **[From Individuals to Interactions: Benchmarking Gender Bias in Multimodal Large Language Models from the Lens of Social Relationship](https://arxiv.org/abs/2506.23101v2)** | 2026-10-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Ensuring the trustworthiness of Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) requires rigorous evaluation of gender bias, particularly in socially sensitive domains. Experiments on six recent MLLMs reveal persistent yet strongly relationship- and model-dependent gender disparities, with some patterns becoming more pronounced in interpersonal settings. We further evaluate two complementary generation-stage mitigation strategies. Our findings demonstrate the value of relationship-aware evaluation for uncovering subtle, interaction-driven gender bias in MLLMs.</p></details> | Accepted by COLM2026 |
| **[PhysElite: How Far Are LLMs from Solving Olympiad-Level Physics Problems?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.25097v3)** | 2026-10-12 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Understanding how (multimodal) large language models perform on physics problems requires benchmarks that reflect the difficulty and breadth of expert-level physical reasoning. Existing physics benchmarks remain limited in the following two important ways: (1) short of high-difficulty datasets, and (2) lack of comprehensive coverage of visual forms, knowledge points, and step-by-step solution processes. As a result, model performance on current datasets may not be fully representative of their ability to solve complex physics problems. To address these issues, we present PhysElite, a large-scale bilingual multimodal benchmark for Olympiad-level physics reasoning. PhysElite contains 11,586 Olympiad-tier problems. For each problem, we provide corresponding visual diagrams, step-by-step bilingual Chinese-English solution derivations, and the final answer. We benchmark 18 open-source and closed-source MLLMs, and find that even the strongest model reaches only 33.7% answer accuracy. We additionally conduct step-level process evaluation to diagnose where models fail in the reasoning chain. Our datasets are released at https://huggingface.co/datasets/physelite/PhysElite.</p></details> | <details><summary>Annua...</summary><p>Annual Conference on Neural Information Processing Systems (NeurIPS) 2026</p></details> |
| **[HERO: Hierarchical Evidential Reasoning Optimization for Radiology Report Generation via Reason-then-Summarize](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.03321v5)** | 2026-10-12 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) have substantially advanced Radiology Report Generation (RRG), yet aligning them through reinforcement learning (RL) remains challenging due to heterogeneous medical supervision. Vanilla Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) assigns uniform credit across the entire generation, leading to segment interference, token dilution, and rationale-diagnosis decoupling, which exacerbates clinical hallucinations. HERO separately optimizes reasoning, diagnosis, and rationale faithfulness through complementary segment-, token-, and completion-level optimization with a heterogeneous reward formulation covering diagnostic accuracy, reasoning quality, and think-answer consistency. Experiments on MIMIC-CXR and IU-Xray show that HERO outperforms strong supervised and reinforcement learning baselines, achieving state-of-the-art clinical efficacy while producing more rationale-supported and think-answer-consistent reports, thereby substantially mitigating clinical hallucinations.</p></details> |  |
| **[Toward Fine-Grained Forgetting:Attribute Unlearning for Multimodal Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.01008v2)** | 2026-10-12 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) can memorize and disclose sensitive information, motivating machine unlearning to remove designated knowledge while preserving model utility. Existing privacy-oriented benchmarks mainly study profile-level deletion, whereas practical requests often require finer-grained forgetting: removing a sensitive attribute while retaining other information about the same identity. Our evaluation shows that target and retain attributes share identity-specific and visual evidence, making selective forgetting prone to residual leakage and collateral degradation. To address this challenge, we propose Causal Localization and Retain-Aware Projection (CLRP), a lightweight, training-free framework that localizes causally relevant layers and projects intermediate representations away from target-specific subspaces while preserving retain-related information. Experiments across MLLMs with different architectures and scales show that CLRP achieves a strong forgetting--retention trade-off on AMU-Bench. Second, repeating vision or audio data beyond 2x yields negligible benefits. Finally, cross-modal interactions are highly asymmetric: vision strongly interferes with audio, whereas audio's interference with learning text and vision is negligible. ## Open Vocabulary Segmentation - 100 in Total
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |

| **[Training-Free Target-Aware Refinement of 3D Gaussian Language Map](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21619v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Distilling vision-language features into 3D Gaussian Splatting yields a language map that can be queried with arbitrary text. In deployment, however, a small set of user-specified targets must be reliably located, which current distilled maps often miss, while open-vocabulary queryability for other words must remain intact. We propose a training-free, target-aware refinement of an already-distilled language map. Guided by a text-promptable segmenter, the visual embedding inside each detected target region is blended with the target text embedding, a convex combination whose coefficient sets the amount of injected prior, while non-target regions are left untouched. Injecting too much overwrites appearance cues and general queryability, so we seek, for each target, the smallest coefficient that still lets it win, and we obtain every coefficient in closed form, leaving no hyperparameters to tune. We further identify a failure mode of injection in language-goal localization. Text embeddings share a common component, and injecting them makes the standard cosine read-out query-agnostic, which a centering step in the read-out removes. Across three benchmarks, the refinement consistently improves open-vocabulary segmentation, roughly doubling the base mIoU on Replica, and localizes language goals more reliably than the base map. It also transfers as a plug-in to off-the-shelf field-based and object-level language maps and supports a real-world demonstration in which a legged robot navigates to natural-language goals. Project page and code: https://lab-of-ai-and-robotics.github.io/target-aware-langmap/</p></details> | <details><summary>8 pag...</summary><p>8 pages, 6 figures. Project page: https://lab-of-ai-and-robotics.github.io/target-aware-langmap/</p></details> |

Experiments on multiple mammography datasets show that the proposed method consistently improves open-vocabulary segmentation baselines on both base and novel lesion categories, while reducing the gap to fully supervised models that require dense target-domain masks. Walls are recorded only where the camera looked, so a gap in the boundary may be a doorway or a stretch of wall that was never observed; nothing distinguishes the two. Prior methods treat both as passages, merging rooms that should remain separate. We observe that both require the same treatment: a room should not extend across either, so both are closed and need not be distinguished. Such an opening closes under a small amount of boundary growth, and few sightlines cross it, so points in different rooms rarely see one another. We use the first to recover rooms and the second to assign objects to them. Rooms are obtained by Progressively thickening the boundary inward and freezing each free-space region once it becomes enclosed, so every opening seals at its own scale rather than at a radius fixed in advance. | **[RADSeg: Unleashing Parameter and Compute Efficient Zero-Shot Open-Vocabulary Segmentation Using Agglomerative Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.19704v2)** | 2026-04-10 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Open-vocabulary semantic segmentation (OVSS) underpins many vision and robotics tasks that require generalizable semantic understanding.
